Time-dependent estimates can be obtained in the results of this section by rewriting the constants
\rho_{mid}
and
\rho_{out}
defining the exist sections
\Delta^{mid}
and
\Delta^{out}
in terms of the corresponding transition times
T_{mid}
and
T
respectively. Specifically, time-dependent estimates in Lemma
3.14
, Theorem
3.20
Assertion (i) and Theorem
3.22
Assertion (i) are obtained by substituting
\varepsilon^{1/2}\rho_{mid}=-\rho_{in}+\varepsilon T_{mid}
, where
T_{mid}=\varepsilon^{-1}\rho_{in}+O(\varepsilon^{-1/2})
, while time-dependent estimates in Lemma
3.16
, Theorem
3.20
Assertion (ii) and Theorem
3.22
Assertion (ii) are obtained by substituting
\rho_{out}=-\rho_{in}+\varepsilon T
, where
T=\varepsilon^{-1}\rho_{in}+O(\varepsilon^{-1})
. In particular, this yields lower bounds on the delay time
t_{delay}
as measured from
v=0
. From Lemma
3.16
it follows that the delay time for the approximation
\Psi
satisfies
t_{delay}\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\rho_{in}/\sqrt{\kappa_{+}}
, and from Theorem
3.22
Assertion (ii) it follows that the delay time for a solution to the SH equation
u
has
t_{delay}\geq\varepsilon^{-1}\rho_{in}/\sqrt{2\kappa_{+}}
.

4
Formal derivation of the modulation equations via geometric blow-up

In this section we derive the modulation equations presented in Section
3.1
. We start with an approximation
\Psi
in the form proposed in (
44
), and derive the modulation equations for the functions
A_{mj}
by imposing the formal requirement that
\textup{Res}(\Psi)=O(r^{n})
. This procedure can be viewed as a direct extension of the established method for deriving modulation equations for the static SH equation in e.g.
[
82
, Ch. 10]
. The main differences here are the following:

•

The ‘small parameter’
r(\bar{t})\geq 0
is now a variable which depends on time;

•

The geometry is more complicated (we work in a blown-up space);

•

The time and space variables
\bar{x}
and
\bar{t}
depend non-trivially on the state-space via their defining equations in (
15
).

Note that the modulation functions
A_{mj}
are also constrained with respect to
v
and
\varepsilon
via the blow-up map (
24
).

Remark 4.1
.

The formal procedure for deriving modulation equations in both this work and classical modulation theory is similar to the established formal method for approximating center manifolds up to arbitrary but algebraic order; see e.g.
[
39
,
93
]
. This is true despite the fact that the GL-manifold for system (
4
) is not generally a center manifold; recall Remark
3.13
. This close relationship to center manifold theory has also been explored in detail in the context of the Kuramoto-Sivashinsky equation in
[
78
]
.

We write

\Psi(x,t)=r(\bar{t})\psi(x,\bar{t})=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}c_{m}(\bar{x},\bar{t})\mathrm{e}^{imx},\qquad c_{m}(\bar{x},\bar{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r(\bar{t})^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj}(\bar{x},\bar{t}),

(44)

and recall that
I_{N}=\{-N,\ldots,N\}
,
N:=n-1\geq 3
,
\alpha(m):=||m|-1|
and
\tilde{\alpha}(m):=N-\alpha(m)-2\delta_{|m|,1}
. We also impose the reality condition
c_{m}=\overline{c_{-m}}
for all
m\in I_{N}
in order to ensure that
\Psi(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}
, and introduce local coordinate representations in charts
K_{l}
via

\Psi_{l}(x,t)=r_{l}(t_{l})\psi_{l}(x,t_{l})=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}c_{m,l}(x_{l},t_{l})\mathrm{e}^{imx},\qquad c_{m,l}(x_{l},t_{l})=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r_{l}(t_{l})^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,l}(x_{l},t_{l}),

(45)

for
l=1,2,3
, where
x_{l}
and
t_{l}
are defined via
\partial_{t}=r_{l}(t_{l})^{2}\partial_{t_{l}}
and
\partial_{x}=r_{l}(t_{l})\partial_{x_{l}}
(the local coordinate representation of (
15
)).
The change of coordinates maps between charts
\mathcal{K}_{l}
are denoted by
\kappa_{12}:\mathcal{K}_{1}\to\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\kappa_{23}:\mathcal{K}_{2}\to\mathcal{K}_{3}
, and given by

\displaystyle\kappa_{12}:

\displaystyle\psi_{1}=(-v_{2})^{-1/2}\psi_{2},

\displaystyle r_{1}=(-v_{2})^{1/2}r_{2},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}=v_{2}^{-2},

\displaystyle v_{2}<0,

(46)

\displaystyle\kappa_{23}:

\displaystyle\psi_{2}=\varepsilon_{3}^{-1/4}\psi_{3},

\displaystyle v_{2}=\varepsilon_{3}^{-1/2},

\displaystyle r_{2}=\varepsilon_{3}^{1/4}r_{3},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}>0.

The change of coordinates formulae for
\psi_{l}
imply the following formulae for the modulation functions
A_{mj,l}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Modulation functions
A_{mj,l}
in different charts
\mathcal{K}_{l}
are related via

A_{mj,1}=(-v_{2})^{-(\alpha(m)+j)/2}A_{mj,2},\ v_{2}<0,\qquad A_{mj,2}=\varepsilon_{3}^{-(\alpha(m)+j)/4}A_{mj,3},\ \varepsilon_{3}>0.

(47)

The formulae (
47
) hold for each
m\in I_{N}
and corresponding
j\in\{1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)\}
.

Proof.

The definition (
44
) imposes the requirement that
c_{m,l_{1}}=c_{m,l_{2}}
where charts
\mathcal{K}_{l_{1}}
and
\mathcal{K}_{l_{2}}
overlap, for
(l_{1},l_{2})=(1,2)
or
(l_{1},l_{2})=(2,3)
. This yields the requirement

\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r_{l_{1}}^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,l_{1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r_{l_{2}}^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,l_{2}}.

If
(l_{1},l_{2})=(1,2)
then using
r_{1}=(-v_{2})^{1/2}r_{2}
(which is valid for
v_{2}<0
) we obtain

\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}((-v_{2})^{1/2}r_{2})^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r_{2}^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,2},\qquad v_{2}<0,

which is only satisfied for all
r_{2}\in[0,\vartheta]
if
A_{mj,1}=(-v_{2})^{-(\alpha(m)+j)/2}A_{mj,2}
for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)\}
, as required. The case
(l_{1},l_{2})=(2,3)
is similar.
∎

After applying the blow-up map (
24
), the residual (
13
) is given by

\textrm{Res}(r\psi)=-\partial_{t}(r\psi)-(1+\partial_{x}^{2})^{2}(r\psi)+r^{3}\bar{v}\psi-r^{3}\psi^{3}+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\mu(x).

(48)

The aim is to formally minimise (
48
) in powers of
r
. First we expand the cubic nonlinearity via

\Psi^{3}=(r\psi)^{3}=\left(\sum_{m\in I_{N}}c_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx}\right)^{3}=\sum_{m=-3N}^{3N}b_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx},

(49)

where the coefficients
b_{m}
, which are defined via the right-most equality, depend the original coefficients
(c_{m})_{m\in I_{N}}
and therefore on
r
via (
44
). Note also that the
b_{m}
are formally
O(r^{3})
or smaller since
\Psi^{3}=O(r^{3})
. Substituting (
49
) and
\mu(x)=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}\nu_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx}
(recall the definition in (
5
)) into (
13
) leads to

\textrm{Res}(r\psi)=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}-b_{m}+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}\right)\mathrm{e}^{imx}-\sum_{m\in I_{3N}\setminus I_{N}}b_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx},

(50)

where
\mathcal{L}_{m}
is the linear operator defined by

\mathcal{L}_{m}:=-r^{2}\partial_{\bar{t}}-(1-m^{2})^{2}-4irm(1-m^{2})\partial_{\bar{x}}+\bar{v}r^{2}-2r^{2}(1-3m^{2})\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}-4ir^{3}m\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3}-r^{4}\partial_{\bar{x}}^{4}.

(51)

Note that we expanded
-(1+\partial_{x}^{2})^{2}c_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx}
and applied the desingularization (
15
) in order to obtain the expression for
\mathcal{L}_{m}
, which is formulated in terms of the transformed
\bar{t}
and
\bar{x}
. The second sum in (
50
) is
O(r^{n})
.

Now we substitute the power series expressions for
c_{m}
in (
44
) and match in powers of
r
. The aim is to show that
(c_{m})_{m\in I_{N}}
can be chosen such that the first sum in (
50
) is
O(r^{n})
. We first consider the linear part
\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}
. In order to distinguish terms at different orders in
r
we define

\mathcal{L}_{m}:=\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}+r\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}+r^{2}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}+r^{3}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}+r^{4}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)},

where

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}:=-(1-m^{2})^{2},\qquad&\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}:=-4im(1-m^{2})\partial_{\bar{x}},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}:=-\partial_{\bar{t}}-2(1-3m^{2})\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}+\bar{v},\\
&\ \ \ \mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}:=-4im\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}:=-\partial_{\bar{x}}^{4},\end{split}

(52)

for each
m\in I_{N}
(c.f. the operators defined after equation (
26
) in Section
3.1
). Since
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}(r^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj})\neq r^{\alpha(m)+j}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}A_{mj}
due to the
\partial_{\bar{t}}
derivative, we further define
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}(r^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj})=r^{\alpha(m)+j}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{mj}
, where

\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}:=-\partial_{\bar{t}}-r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r-2(1-3m^{2})\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}+\bar{v},

in order to simplify the matching procedures below. We note that
r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r=O(1)
as
r\to 0
, since

\partial_{t_{1}}r_{1}=\frac{1}{2}r_{1}\varepsilon_{1},\qquad\partial_{t_{2}}r_{2}=0,\qquad\partial_{t_{3}}r_{3}=-\frac{1}{2}r_{3}\varepsilon_{3},

in charts
\mathcal{K}_{1}
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}
respectively. With the preceding definitions, the linear term
\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}=&\left(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}+r\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}+r^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}+r^{3}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}+r^{4}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}\right)\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj}\\
=&\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r^{\alpha(m)+j}\left(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}+r\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}+r^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}+r^{3}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}+r^{4}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}\right)A_{mj},\end{split}

(53)

which is a power series in
r
.

The role of the nonlinearity at each order in
r
can be extracted by writing
b_{m}

as power series in
r
, i.e.

b_{m}=\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}a_{mj}r^{\alpha(m)+j},\qquad m\in I_{N}.

(54)

The
a_{mj}
have an explicit formula which can be derived using Cauchy products if necessary, and do not depend on
r
. For our present purposes, it suffices to note that each
a_{mj}
is a finite sum of cubic monomials of the form
lA_{m_{1}j_{1}}A_{m_{2}j_{2}}A_{m_{3}j_{3}}
, where
l\in\mathbb{N}
, and the
m_{i}\in I_{N}
and
j_{i}\in\{0,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m_{i})\}
satisfy the matching conditions

•

m=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
;

•

\alpha(m)+j+2=\alpha(m_{1})+\alpha(m_{2})+\alpha(m_{3})+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}
;

which follow directly from the definitions of the coefficients
(c_{k})_{k\in I_{N}}
,
(b_{k})_{k\in I_{3N}}
and
(a_{k})_{k\in I_{3N}}
in (
44
), (
49
) and (
54
) respectively. Specifically, the former condition comes from matching
\mathrm{e}^{imx}
with
\mathrm{e}^{i(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})x}
, while the latter comes from matching
r^{\alpha(m_{1})+\alpha(m_{2})+\alpha(m_{3})+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}}
with
r^{\alpha(m)+j+2}
(the ‘
+2
’ appears because
b_{m}=O(r^{3})
). In what follows we shall often appeal to the fact that
a_{mj}
only depends on the critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j}
and the lower order modulation functions
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
for which
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<\alpha(m)+j
. The following property in the case of critical modes with
|m|=1
will also be helpful.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
j\geq 2
then
a_{\pm 1j}
depends linearly on
A_{\pm 1j}
, i.e. the maps
A_{\pm 1j}\mapsto a_{\pm 1j}
and
A_{\mp 1j}\mapsto a_{\pm 1j}
are linear.

Proof.

Let
m\in\{-1,1\}
. Then
\alpha(m)=0
and therefore

\alpha(m_{1})+\alpha(m_{2})+\alpha(m_{3})+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}=j+2.

Assume that
j_{1}=j\geq 2
, i.e. that
a_{\pm 1j}
depends on
A_{1j}
or
A_{-1j}
. Then

\alpha(m_{1})+\alpha(m_{2})+\alpha(m_{3})+j_{2}+j_{3}=2,

implying that
\alpha(m_{1})=\alpha(m_{2})=\alpha(m_{3})=0
and
j_{2}=j_{3}=1
. Thus
a_{\pm 1j}
is a linear combination of cubic monomials
A_{m_{1}j}A_{m_{2}1}A_{m_{3}1}
where
m_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
for each
i=1,2,3
. Hence
A_{\pm 1j}\mapsto a_{\pm 1j}
and
A_{\mp 1j}\mapsto a_{\pm 1j}
are linear maps.
∎

In particular, the proof of Lemma
4.3
implies that if
j\geq 2
the
a_{\pm 1j}
has the form
a_{\pm 1j}=:a_{\pm 1j}^{L}+a_{\pm 1j}^{N}
, where

a_{\pm 1j}^{L}(\tilde{A}_{1j},\overline{\tilde{A}_{1j}})=\left(\alpha_{1}A_{11}^{2}+\alpha_{2}|A_{11}|^{2}+\alpha_{3}\overline{A_{11}}^{2}\right)A_{1j}+\left(\beta_{1}A_{11}^{2}+\beta_{2}|A_{11}|^{2}+\beta_{3}\overline{A_{11}}^{2}\right)\overline{A_{1j}},

(55)

where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
are non-negative integers, and
a_{j}^{N}
depends only modulation functions
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
at lower orders with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<j
.

Using (
53
) and (
54
), the coefficients in the first sum in (
50
) can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}-b_{m}&+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}=\\
&\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r^{\alpha(m)+j}\left(\big(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}+r\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}+r^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}+r^{3}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}+r^{4}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}\big)A_{mj}-a_{mj}\right)+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m},\end{split}

(56)

for each
m\in I_{N}
. In the following, modulation equations are derived by choosing
A_{mj}
so that the coefficients of (
56
) vanish at each order in
r
, thereby (formally) minimising the residual (
50
). The critical case
|m|=1
is distinguished by the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{0}\equiv 0
and
\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{1}\equiv 0
. We consider this case first.

Critical modulation equations for
m=\pm 1

For
|m|=1
we have
m\in\{-1,1\}
,
\alpha(m)=0
and
\tilde{\alpha}(m)=N-2
. Equation (
56
) becomes

\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}-b_{m}+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}=\sum_{j=1}^{N-2}r^{j}\left(\big(r^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}+r^{3}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}+r^{4}\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}\big)A_{mj}-a_{mj}\right)+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}.

(57)

Matching in powers of
r
and requiring (
57
) to vanish at each order leads to the following
N-2
equations:

\begin{split}O(r^{3}):\ &\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(2)}}A_{\pm 11}-a_{\pm 11}=0,\\
O(r^{4}):\ &\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(2)}}A_{\pm 12}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(3)}A_{\pm 11}-a_{\pm 12}+\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{\pm 1}=0,\\
O(r^{5}):\ &\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(2)}}A_{\pm 13}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(3)}A_{\pm 12}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(4)}A_{\pm 11}-a_{\pm 13}=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\vdots\\
O(r^{j}):\ &\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(2)}}A_{\pm 1j-2}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(3)}A_{\pm 1j-3}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(4)}A_{\pm 1j-4}-a_{\pm 1j-2}=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\vdots\\
O(r^{N}):\ &\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(2)}}A_{\pm 1N-2}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(3)}A_{\pm 1N-3}+\mathcal{L}_{\pm 1}^{(4)}A_{\pm 1N-4}-a_{\pm 1N-2}=0.\end{split}

(58)

For our purposes it will suffice to write the first two equations explicitly. Direct calculations show that
a_{\pm 11}=3A_{\pm 11}|A_{\pm 11}|^{2}
and
a_{\pm 12}=3A_{\pm 11}^{2}A_{\mp 12}+6|A_{\pm 11}|^{2}A_{\pm 12}
. Hence at
O(r^{3})
we obtain a GL equation

\partial_{\bar{t}}A_{\pm 11}=4\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}A_{\pm 11}+\left(\bar{v}-r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r\right)A_{\pm 11}-3A_{\pm 11}|A_{\pm 11}|^{2},

(59)

where
\bar{v}-r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r
can be considered as a time-dependent coefficient (assuming that the equations for
\bar{v}(\bar{t})
,
r(\bar{t})
and
\bar{\varepsilon}(\bar{t})
can be solved explicitly in each chart
\mathcal{K}_{l}
,
l=1,2,3
). Assuming the existence of solutions
A_{\pm 11}(\bar{x},\bar{t})
, the equation at
O(r^{4})
is

\partial_{\bar{t}}A_{\pm 12}=\ 4\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}A_{\pm 12}+\left(\bar{v}-r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r\right)A_{\pm 12}-3A_{\pm 11}^{2}A_{\mp 12}-6|A_{\pm 11}|^{2}A_{\pm 12}\mp 4i\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3}A_{\pm 11}+\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{\pm 1},

(60)

which takes the form of a linearized GL-type equation with a source term and coefficients depending on both time and space.

Assuming the existence of solutions
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(\bar{x},\bar{t})
at lower orders with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j_{1}\leq\alpha(m)+j=j
(recall that
\alpha(m)=0
if
m=\pm 1
), the remaining equations are given by

\partial_{\bar{t}}A_{\pm 1j}=\ 4\partial_{\bar{x}}^{2}A_{\pm 1j}+\left(\bar{v}-r^{-1}\partial_{\bar{t}}r\right)A_{\pm 1j}-a_{\pm 1j}\mp 4i\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3}A_{\pm 1j-1}-\partial_{\bar{x}}^{4}A_{\pm 1j-2},

(61)

for
j\in\{3,\ldots,N-2\}
, where we recall by Lemma
4.3
that
a_{\pm 1j}
is linear in
A_{\pm 1j}
and otherwise dependent only on lower order solutions
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime}}(\bar{x},\bar{t})
. Since we assume the existence of solutions at lower orders, equation (
61
) can also be seen as a linearized GL-type equation with a source term and coefficients depending on both time and space. Equations (
59
), (
60
) and (
61
) are precisely those presented in system (
25
), Section
3.1
.

Remark 4.4
.

Due to the recursive structure of the derivation of equations (
59
), (
60
) and (
61
), the existence of solutions the entire system of equations in (
18
) can be established on suitable time intervals by a recursive argument relying only on the existence of solutions to the lowest order GL-type equation (
59
). This follows from the results of Section
5
. A similar result applies for the static GL reduction in
[
82
, Ch. 10]
.

Remark 4.5
.

Due to the presence of derivatives in the higher order equations with
j\geq 2
, the required regularity increases in proportion to the order of the approximation
n
. As in the static theory, see e.g.
[
82
, Rem. 10.2.3]
, the most regularity is lost as a result of the
\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3}A_{\pm 1j-1}
term. Specifically, we require
A_{\pm 12}(\cdot,\bar{t})\in H_{ul}^{4}
to estimate
\partial_{\bar{x}}^{4}A_{\pm 12}(\cdot,\bar{t})
, which imposes the requirement that
A_{\pm 11}(\cdot,\bar{t})\in H_{ul}^{7}
because we do not take the smoothing properties of the semigroup into account. For higher order approximations we have to assume that solutions to (
59
) satisfy
A_{\pm 11}(\cdot,\bar{t})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
on the relevant time interval, where
\theta_{A}:=3(n-3)+1
. This suffices to compensate the loss of three derivatives at each step due to the
\partial_{\bar{x}}^{3}A_{\pm 1j-1}
term.

Non-critical modulation equations for
m\neq\pm 1

Now assume that
|m|\neq 1
. In this case
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}
are non-trivial. In particular,
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}
is invertible with

(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)})^{-1}=-(1-m^{2})^{-2}\neq 0.

We obtain the following equations after matching:

\begin{split}O(r^{\alpha(m)+1}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m1}-a_{m1}+\delta_{|m|,4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}=0,\\
O(r^{\alpha(m)+2}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{m1}-a_{m2}+\delta_{|m|,3}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}=0,\\
O(r^{\alpha(m)+3}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m3}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{m2}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{m1}-a_{m3}+(\delta_{|m|,0}+\delta_{|m|,2})\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}=0,\\
O(r^{\alpha(m)+4}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m4}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{m3}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{m2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}A_{m1}-a_{m4}=0,\\
O(r^{\alpha(m)+5}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m5}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{m4}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{m3}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}A_{m2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}A_{m1}-a_{m5}=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\vdots\\
O(r^{\alpha(m)+j}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{mj}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{mj-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{mj-2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}A_{mj-3}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}A_{mj-4}-a_{mj}=0,\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\vdots\\
O(r^{N}):\ &\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}A_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)-2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}A_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)-3}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}A_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)-4}-a_{m\tilde{\alpha}(m)}=0.\end{split}

Since
\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)}
is invertible for each
m\neq\pm 1
, the above may written more concisely as

\begin{split}A_{mj}=-(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(0)})^{-1}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(1)}A_{mj-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(2)}}A_{mj-2}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(3)}A_{mj-3}+\mathcal{L}_{m}^{(4)}A_{mj-4}-a_{mj}+\delta_{\alpha(m)+j,4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}\bigg),\end{split}

where we define
A_{mj-k}\equiv 0
if
j-k\leq 0
, which is precisely equation (
26
) from Section
3.1
. Assuming the existence of solutions at lower orders
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<\alpha(m)+j
(including
A_{mj-1}
,
A_{mj-2}
,
A_{mj-3}
and
A_{mj-4}
), the existence of a solution
A_{mj}
with
|m|\neq 1
is given by the
algebraic equation
(
26
), which is used to define the GL-manifold
\mathcal{M}^{gl}
. For clarity we provide the explicit form of the lowest order approximation when
n=4
. We have

A_{01}=A_{02}=A_{\pm 21}=A_{\pm 22}\equiv 0,\qquad A_{\pm 31}=-\frac{A_{\pm 11}^{3}}{64},

(62)

such that the fourth order approximation is

\Psi_{4}(x,t)=r(\bar{t})\left(A_{11}(\bar{x},\bar{t})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)-\frac{r(\bar{t})^{3}}{64}\left(A_{11}(\bar{x},\bar{t})^{3}\mathrm{e}^{3ix}+c.c.\right).

The approximation at order
n\geq 5
is therefore formally

\Psi_{n}(x,t)=r(\bar{t})\left(A_{11}(\bar{x},\bar{t})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)+r(\bar{t})^{2}\left(A_{12}(\bar{x},\bar{t})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)-\frac{r(\bar{t})^{3}}{64}\left(A_{11}(\bar{x},\bar{t})^{3}\mathrm{e}^{3ix}+c.c.\right)+O(r^{4}).

Remark 4.6
.

The recursive structure of the approximation is such that the algebraic equations for non-critical modulation functions
A_{mj}
with
m\neq\pm 1
can be written as a graph over lower order critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}<\alpha(m)+j
, similarly to the representation of hyperbolic variables as a graph over the center variables in a center manifold reduction. In particular, the nonlinear term
a_{mj}
is linear in
A_{\pm 1j}
(recall Lemma
4.3
) and otherwise dependent only on lower order critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime}}
with
j^{\prime}<j
.

For the remainder of this work we shall assume that the approximation
\Psi
is given by (
44
), with
A_{\pm 1j}
defined by equations (
25
) and
A_{mj}
with
|m|\neq 1
defined by equations (
26
). For this choice of ansatz we have formally that

\textrm{Res}(r\psi)=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}\left(\mathcal{L}_{m}c_{m}-b_{m}+r^{4}\bar{\varepsilon}\nu_{m}\right)\mathrm{e}^{imx}-\sum_{m\in I_{3N}\setminus I_{N}}b_{m}\mathrm{e}^{imx}=O(r^{n}),

since by construction the first term vanishes up to and including
O(r^{N})
and the second term is
O(r^{n})
. The corresponding systems of modulation equations in local coordinate charts
\mathcal{K}_{1}
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}
are described by Propositions
3.10
,
3.11
and
3.12
respectively. We turn now to the dynamics of the modulation equations in charts.

5
Dynamics in charts and proofs for Lemmas
3.14
-
3.16

In this section we prove Lemmas
3.14
and
3.16
, which are necessary for the proofs of both Theorems
3.20
and
3.22
. In order to do so, we analyse the dynamics of the approximation
\Psi=r\psi
in charts
K_{l}
,
l=1,2,3
.

Our aim is to describe the dynamics of
\Psi(x,t)
, in particular the maps
\pi^{mid}:\Delta^{in}\to\Delta^{mid}
and
\pi:\Delta^{in}\to\Delta^{out}
induced by forward evolution under system (
4
) up to time
T_{mid}=\varepsilon^{-1}(\rho_{in}+\varepsilon^{1/2}\rho_{mid})
and
T=\varepsilon^{-1}(\rho_{in}+\rho_{out})
respectively; recall Section
3.2
. We further restrict to initial conditions
\Psi(x,0)=\Psi^{\ast}(x)
contained within the blown-down image of the GL manifold
\mathcal{M}^{gl}
, i.e. initial conditions of the form

\Psi^{\ast}(x)=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}|v(0)|^{(\alpha(m)+j)/2}A_{mj}^{\ast}(|v(0)|^{1/2}x)\mathrm{e}^{imx},

where
x\in\mathbb{R}
, subject to the requirement
\|\Psi^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\rho_{in}^{1/2}K
for a small but fixed (with respect to
\varepsilon
) constant
K>0
. We analyse the maps
\pi^{mid}
and
\pi
via their pre-image after blow-up as a composition of three component maps
\pi_{l}
(one for each chart
\mathcal{K}_{l}
).

5.1
Chart
\mathcal{K}_{1}

We recall the equations in chart
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, i.e.

\begin{split}\partial_{t_{1}}A_{1,1}&=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{1,1}+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\right)A_{1,1}-3A_{1,1}|A_{1,1}|^{2},\\
\partial_{t_{1}}r_{1}&=-\frac{1}{2}r_{1}\varepsilon_{1},\\
\partial_{t_{1}}\varepsilon_{1}&=2\varepsilon_{1}^{2},\\
\partial_{t_{1}}A_{2,1}&=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{2,1}+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\right)A_{2,1}-a_{2,1}-4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,1}+\varepsilon_{1}\nu_{1},\\
\partial_{t_{1}}A_{j,1}&=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{j,1}+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}\right)A_{j,1}-a_{j,1}-4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1},\end{split}

(63)

where
j=3,\ldots,N-2
. Our aim is to describe the map

\pi_{1}:\Sigma^{in}_{1}\ni\left(\psi_{1}^{\ast},\rho_{in}^{1/2},\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}\right)\mapsto\left(\psi_{1}(\cdot,T_{1}),r_{1}^{out}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}),\zeta\right)\in\Sigma_{1}^{out}

(64)

induced by forward evolution of initial conditions in

\Sigma_{1}^{in}=\left\{(\psi_{1}^{\ast},\rho_{in}^{1/2},\varepsilon_{1}):\|\psi_{1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq K,\varepsilon_{1}\in[0,\zeta]\right\},

which
may be viewed as the blown-up preimage of the entry section
\Delta^{in}
(or more precisely
\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{in}
; recall the definition in (
35
) and the discussion which follows it)
, up to the exit section

\Sigma_{1}^{out}=\left\{(\psi_{1},r_{1},\zeta):\|\psi_{1}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq K,r_{1}\in[0,\rho_{in}^{1/2}]\right\}.

Note that
\Sigma^{1}_{in}
contains the relevant initial conditions since
\|\Psi^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}=\rho_{in}^{1/2}\|\psi_{1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\rho_{in}^{1/2}K
. The analysis here and in charts
\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}
proceeds in a number of steps. Since the structure of the argument remains the same in each chart, it is instructive to summarise the argument for
\mathcal{K}_{1}
before continuing with the details. The steps are as follows:

I

Solve the equations for
r_{1}(t_{1})
,
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
and the transition time
T_{1}
taken for solutions to reach
\Sigma_{1}^{out}
.

II

Substitute the solution for
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
into the equation for
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
in order to obtain a (decoupled) GL-type equation with time-dependent linear decay. Use this equation to estimate
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
.

III

Given a solution for
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
and an estimate for
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
, the equation for
A_{2,1}
can be viewed as a (decoupled) linear, inhomogeneous GL-type equation with
(x_{1},t_{1})
-dependent decay and a source term. Estimate
A_{2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
using linear theory.

IV

Proceed iteratively with the equations for
A_{j,1}
,
j\geq 3
, which may be viewed as (decoupled) linear inhomogeneous GL-type equations depending on
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
and (previously solved for)
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime},1}(x_{1},t_{1})
with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<j
. Estimate each
A_{j,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
using linear theory.

V

Use the solutions and estimates obtained in Steps I-IV to estimate
\psi_{1}(x,t_{1})
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
.

Remark 5.1
.

Throughout this section and the remainder of this manuscript we shall frequently denote different positive constants by
C
in cases where the exact expression is unimportant or there is no need to distinguish it from other constants. Constants denoted by
C
are understood to be independent of small parameters such as
\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}
.

Step I: Solve for
r_{1}(t_{1})
,
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
and
T_{1}

Recall that
\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}:=\varepsilon_{1}(0)
. We have the following result.

Lemma 5.2
.

The transition time is given by

T_{1}=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}{\zeta}\right),

and for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
we have

r_{1}(t_{1})=\rho_{in}^{1/2}(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1})^{1/4},\qquad\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}.

In particular,
r_{1}^{out}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})=\rho_{in}^{1/2}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}/\zeta)^{1/4}
.

Proof.

The expressions for
r_{1}(t_{1})
and
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
follow by direct integration of the corresponding equations in (
63
), and
T_{1}
is obtained from the requirement that
\varepsilon_{1}(T_{1})=\zeta
. The expression for
r_{1}^{out}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})
follows from
r_{1}^{out}(\varepsilon_{1})=r_{1}(T_{1})
.
∎

Step II: Estimate
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})

We now show that solutions
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
are exponentially attracted to the trivial homogeneous solution in
H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in(0,T_{1}]
.

Remark 5.3
.

In the following we assume that
A_{1,1}^{\ast}\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
and specify the degree of regularity
\theta_{A}
as we go. By Remark
4.5
, we expect that a higher degree regularity is necessary in each successive step of the approximation.

Lemma 5.4
.

Fix
\theta_{A}>1/2
and assume that
A_{1,1}^{\ast}\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
. Then
A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, and

\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}},\qquad t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}],

where
\tilde{c}\in(0,1)
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
1
by choosing
K>0
sufficiently small. In particular, for each fixed
\kappa\in(0,\tilde{c})
, if
\zeta>0
is sufficiently small then

\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}.

(65)

Proof.

Substituting the expression for
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
in Lemma
5.2
into the equation for
A_{1,1}
in (
63
) yields a GL-type equation with non-constant linear damping:

\partial_{t_{1}}A_{1,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{1,1}+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})}{2}\right)A_{1,1}-3A_{1,1}|A_{1,1}|^{2},\qquad A_{1,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{1,1}^{\ast}(x_{1}).

(66)

In order to control the time-dependent coefficient
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})/2
we introduce an integrating factor
\mathrm{e}^{I(t_{1})}
, where

I(t_{1})=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\left(-\eta+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{*}}{2(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{*}s_{1})}\right)ds_{1}=-\eta t_{1}-\frac{1}{4}\ln(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{*}t_{1}),\qquad\mathrm{e}^{I(t_{1})}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\eta t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}},

where
\eta>0
is arbitrarily small but fixed, and define the transformation
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})=:\mathrm{e}^{I(t_{1})}\tilde{A}_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
. This leads to an equation with constant linear decay, namely

\displaystyle\partial_{t_{1}}\tilde{A}_{1,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{1,1}-(1-\eta)\tilde{A}_{1,1}-3\mathrm{e}^{2I(t_{1})}\tilde{A}_{1,1}|\tilde{A}_{1,1}|^{2},\qquad\tilde{A}_{1,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{1,1}^{\ast}(x_{1}).

(67)

Variation of constants yields

\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})=\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1}}A_{1,1}^{\ast}-3\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-s_{1})\Lambda_{1,1}}\mathrm{e}^{2I(s_{1})}\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|^{2}ds_{1},

(68)

where
(\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1}})_{t_{1}\geq 0}
is the semigroup generated by
\Lambda_{1,1}:=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-(1-\eta)
. In order to estimate the norm of
\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})
on
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, we need estimates for the semigroup and the cubic nonlinearity
\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|^{2}
.

The semigroup can be bounded using Fourier multiplier techniques. Specifically, we use
[
82
, Lemma 8.3.7]
(see also
[
73
]
); see Lemma
A.2
in Appendix
A
for a reformulation in the present notation. Since for each
c\in(0,1)
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\|\xi_{1}\mapsto\exp(-(4\xi_{1}^{2}+1)t_{1})\|_{C^{2}_{b}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-(c-\eta)t_{1}},

Lemma
A.2
implies that for each
c\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C>0
depending on
{\theta_{A}}
such that

\|\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1,1}}\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}\to H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-(c-\eta)t_{1}}.

If we use the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{2I(t_{1})}=\mathrm{e}^{-2\eta t_{1}}(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1})^{-1/2}\leq C
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, it remains to control the nonlinear term
\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|^{2}
. For
\tilde{K}>K>0
, define

\tilde{T}_{1}:=\min\left\{T_{1},\sup\{t_{1}>0:\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq\tilde{K}\}\right\}

(69)

such that
A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{1}]
. We use the fact that
H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
is an algebra for
{\theta_{A}}>1/2
. Using
[
82
, Lemma 8.3.11]
(see Lemma
A.3
in Appendix
A
for a formulation in the present notation) we obtain

\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|^{2}\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}\leq C\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}^{3}\leq C\tilde{K}^{2}\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}},

for all
s_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{1}]
.

Using the bounds obtained above and equation (
68
) we obtain

\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-(c-\eta)t_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}+C\tilde{K}^{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-(c-\eta)(t_{1}-s_{1})}\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}ds_{1},

for
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{1}]
. Applying the Grönwall inequality in
[
42
, Lemma 2.8]
(see also Lemma
A.4
in Appendix
A
) yields

\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-(\tilde{c}-\eta)t_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}},\qquad t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{1}],

(70)

where
\tilde{c}:=c-C\tilde{K}^{2}
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
c
if
K
is chosen sufficiently small (
\tilde{K}>K
can be chosen arbitrarily close to
K
). Choosing
K
and
\eta
sufficiently small so that
\tilde{c}-\eta>0
ensures that
\tilde{T}_{1}=T_{1}
, i.e. that (
70
) holds for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Thus we obtain the desired bounds via

\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}=\mathrm{e}^{I(t_{1})}\|\tilde{A}_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{*}t_{1}}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}},

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, and

\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}},

(71)

for any fixed
\kappa\in(0,\tilde{c})
, as long as
\zeta>0
is fixed and sufficiently small.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In many of the proofs below we make use of definitions similar to that for
\tilde{T}_{1}
in equation (
69
). Specifically, we shall denote by
\tilde{T}_{j,l}
the time

\tilde{T}_{j,l}:=\min\left\{T_{l},\sup\{t_{l}>0:\|\tilde{A}_{j,l}(\cdot,t_{l})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\tilde{K}\}\right\}

in chart
\mathcal{K}_{l}
, where the constant
\tilde{K}>K>0
is fixed but arbitrarily close to
K
. In cases where
\tilde{K}
must be larger (i.e. not close to
K
), we use
\tilde{M}
instead of
\tilde{K}
to denote an arbitrarily large but fixed positive constant. In both cases,
\tilde{A}_{j,l}(\cdot,t_{l})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{l}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{l}]
, by definition.

Step III: Estimate
A_{2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})

We prove the following result.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\theta_{A}=3+\theta
where
\theta>1/2
. Then
A_{2,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Moreover, we have that

A_{2,1}(x_{1},T_{1})=\nu_{1}f_{2,1}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})+A_{2,1,h}(x_{1},T_{1}),

(72)

where

f_{2,1}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})=\mathrm{e}^{1/2\zeta}\sqrt[4]{\frac{\zeta}{8}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2\zeta}\right)+O({\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{3/4}\mathrm{e}^{-1/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}})\approx\zeta+O({\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{3/4}\mathrm{e}^{-1/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}})

as
\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}\to 0
, and

\|A_{2,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{3/4}}\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}},

(73)

where
\tilde{c}\in(0,1)
is the same constant as in Lemma
5.4
and

\theta_{k}:=\begin{cases}\theta_{A},&k=1,\\
\theta,&k=2.\end{cases}

(74)

Proof.

Given solutions for
\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})
and
A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
as described in Lemmas
5.2
and
5.4
respectively, the equation for
A_{2,1}
decouples from system (
63
). We obtain a linear inhomogeneous GL-type equation with a non-constant coefficient and a time-varying source, namely

\begin{split}\partial_{t_{1}}A_{2,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{2,1}&+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})}{2}\right)A_{2,1}\\
&-3A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})^{2}\overline{A_{2,1}}-6|A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})|^{2}A_{2,1}-4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})+\nu_{1}\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1}),\end{split}

with
A_{2,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{2,1}^{\ast}(x_{1})
for
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
and
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. Similarly to the analysis in Step II, we define an integrating factor

I_{1}(t_{1})=\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{*}}{2(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{*}s_{1})}ds_{1}=-\frac{1}{4}\ln(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{*}t_{1}),\qquad\mathrm{e}^{I(t_{1})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}},

and apply a transformation
A_{2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})=:\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(t_{1})}\tilde{A}_{2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
to obtain

\begin{split}\partial_{t_{1}}\tilde{A}_{2,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,1}-\tilde{A}_{2,1}-3A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})^{2}\overline{\tilde{A}_{2,1}}&-6|A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})|^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,1}\\
&-4i\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(t_{1})}\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})+\nu_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(t_{1})}\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1}),\end{split}

with
\tilde{A}_{2,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{2,1}^{\ast}(x_{1})
. After applying the variation of constants formula, the solution may be split into two parts by setting
\tilde{A}_{2,1}:=\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}+\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}
, where

\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})&=\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1}}A_{2,1}^{\ast}-\\
3\int_{0}^{t_{1}}&\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-s_{1})\Lambda_{1}}\left(A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})^{2}\overline{\tilde{A}_{2,1}}(\cdot,s_{1})+2|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})|^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})-4i\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(s_{1})}\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\right)ds_{1},\\
\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})&=\nu_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-s_{1})\Lambda_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(s_{1})}\varepsilon_{1}(s_{1})ds_{1},\end{split}

(75)

and
\Lambda_{1}:=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}-1
. In the following we derive a bound in
H_{ul}^{\theta}
by defining
A_{2,1,h}:=\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}}\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}
,
A_{2,1,s}:=\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}}\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}
, and considering each part separately.

We first consider
\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}
.
A bound for the semigroup can be derived using Lemma
A.2
, as in Step II. In this case we find that for all
c\in(0,1)
there is a constant
C>0
depending on
\theta
such that
\|\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1}}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}\to H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}
. It follows that

\begin{split}\|\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\|A_{2,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&+C\tilde{K}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-c(t_{1}-s_{1})}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}ds_{1}\\
&+C\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-c(t_{1}-s_{1})}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(s_{1})}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}ds_{1},\end{split}

for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2,1}]
, where we used Lemma
A.3
and the fact that
\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\tilde{K}
in order to derive the first integral, and the fact that

A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}\ \implies\ \|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+3}}=C\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}},

in order to derive the second (this step explains the requirement
\theta_{A}=3+\theta
). Both integrals can be bounded using Lemma
5.4
. We have

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-c(t_{1}-s_{1})}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}ds_{1}&=\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{cs_{1}}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}ds_{1}\\
&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(2\tilde{c}-c)s_{1}}}{\sqrt{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}ds_{1},\end{split}

(76)

and

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-c(t_{1}-s_{1})}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(s_{1})}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}ds_{1}&=\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{cs_{1}}\sqrt{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}^{1/4}\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}ds_{1}\\
&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(c-\tilde{c})s_{1}}ds_{1}\\
&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}},\end{split}

(77)

for
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2,1}]
. Note that
c-\tilde{c}\in(0,1)
and
2\tilde{c}-c=2-2C\tilde{K}^{2}\in(0,1)
as long as
\tilde{K}>K>0
is chosen sufficiently small. Using Lemma
A.5
and the gamma function asymptotics in Appendix
A
(equation (
166
)) to evaluate the integral in (
76
), we obtain

\begin{split}\|\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\|A_{2,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}+C\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\\
&+Ci\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-(2\tilde{c}-c)/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\frac{\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},-(2\tilde{c}-c)\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}},\\
&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}},\end{split}

(78)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2,1}]
, where
\theta_{k}
is given by (
74
). We therefore have that

\|\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}}.

Since
c
and
\tilde{K}
are chosen so that
\tilde{c}=c-C\tilde{K}^{2}\in(0,1)
we can set
\tilde{T}_{2,1}=T_{1}
. It follows that

\|A_{2,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}=\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(T_{1})}\|\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{3/4}}\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}},

thereby proving the desired bound (
73
).

The equation for
\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})
can be solved explicitly, since
\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}
solves a heat equation with linear damping:

\partial_{t_{1}}\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}-\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}+\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(t_{1})}\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})\nu_{1},\qquad\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}(x_{1},0)\equiv 0.

(79)

Using the explicit solution formula for the heat equation, see e.g.
[
34
, Ch. 2.3]
, we obtain

\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}(x_{1},t_{1})&=\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}\nu_{1}}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s_{1}}}{(t_{1}-s_{1})^{1/2}(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{3/4}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-(x_{1}-\xi_{1})^{2}/16(t_{1}-s_{1})}d\xi_{1}ds_{1}\\
&=\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}\nu_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{s_{1}}}{(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{3/4}}ds_{1}\\
&=\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\nu_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{1/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{2^{3/4}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}},\end{split}

(80)

for all
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, where we applied Lemma
A.5
again in order to obtain the final expression. Hence

\begin{split}A_{2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})&=\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(t_{1})}\left(\tilde{A}_{2,1,s}(x_{1},t_{1})+\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(x_{1},t_{1})\right)\\
&=\nu_{1}\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}+1/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{2^{3/4}\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\tilde{A}_{2,1,h}(x_{1},t_{1}),\end{split}

for all
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Substituting the expression for
t_{1}=T_{1}
in Lemma
5.2
and applying the known asymptotic formula for the incomplete gamma function in Appendix
A
(equation (
166
))
yields the expression in (
72
).
∎

Step IV: Estimate
A_{j,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
,
j=3,\ldots,N-2

We now consider modulation functions
A_{j,1}
with
j\geq 3
. We recall from Section
4
, in particular Lemma
4.3
, that the linear
A_{j,1}
-dependent part of the
a_{j,1}
term in the equation for
A_{j,1}
in (
63
) can be extracted by setting
a_{j,1}=:a_{j,1}^{L}+a_{j,1}^{N}
, where

a_{j,1}^{L}(\tilde{A}_{j,1},\overline{\tilde{A}_{j,1}})=\left(\alpha_{1}A_{1,1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}|A_{1,1}|^{2}+\alpha_{3}\overline{A_{1,1}}^{2}\right)\tilde{A}_{j,1}+\left(\beta_{1}A_{1,1}^{2}+\beta_{2}|A_{1,1}|^{2}+\beta_{3}\overline{A_{1,1}}^{2}\right)\overline{\tilde{A}_{j,1}},

(81)

where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
and
\beta_{3}
are non-negative integers, and
a_{j,1}^{N}
depends only modulation functions
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime},1}
at lower orders with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<j
.

We prove the following result.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\theta_{A}=\theta_{A}(j)=3(j-1)+\theta
where
j\in\{3,\ldots,N-2\}
and
\theta>1/2
, and assume that
a_{j,1}^{N}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Then
A_{j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. If additionally
\mu(x)\equiv 0
and

\|a_{j,1}^{N}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\max_{k=1,\ldots,j-1}\|A_{k,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,j}}}^{3},

(82)

then for
K>0
sufficiently small it holds that

\|A_{j,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\max_{k=1,\ldots,j}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,j}}}

(83)

for each fixed
\kappa\in(0,\tilde{c})
, where

\theta_{k,j}:=3(j-k)+\theta=\begin{cases}\theta_{A}(j),&k=1,\\
3(j-k)+\theta,&k=2,\ldots,j-1,\\
\theta,&k=j,\end{cases}

(84)

extends the definition of
\theta_{k}
in (
74
).

Proof.

Since the structure of the equation for
A_{j,1}
in (
63
) is the same for each
j\geq 3
, we can show that
A_{j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
by an inductive argument over
k=3,\ldots,j-1\leq N-3
. We omit an explicit treatment of the base case
k=3
, which is simpler and can be treated with minor adaptations of the arguments applied in the induction step below. The induction hypothesis is that
A_{k,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,j}}
for each
k=3,\ldots,j-1
, for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
.

The equation for
A_{j,1}
is given by

\partial_{t_{1}}A_{j,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}A_{j,1}+\left(-1+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})}{2}\right)A_{j,1}-a_{j,1}-4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}-\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1},\quad A_{j,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{j,1}^{\ast}(x_{1}),

where
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
and
a_{j,1}
is given by
a_{j,1}=a_{j,1}^{L}+a_{j,1}^{N}
with
a_{j,1}^{L}
as in (
81
). This equation also decouples from system (
63
), and many of the estimates in
H_{ul}^{\theta}
can be obtained via arguments similar to those used in Steps II-III. We therefore present fewer details. Both
-4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}
and
\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}
may be considered as source terms, since we given solutions
A_{j-1,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
and
A_{j-2,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
for all
x_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
either via either Lemma
5.4
, Lemma
5.6
, or the induction hypothesis (depending on
j
).

Using the same integrating factor as in Step III leads to

\partial_{t_{1}}\tilde{A}_{j,1}=4\partial_{x_{1}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{j,1}-\tilde{A}_{j,1}-a_{j,1}^{L}(\tilde{A}_{j,1},\overline{\tilde{A}_{j,1}})-\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(t_{1})}\left(a^{N}_{j,1}+4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}+\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}\right),

together with
\tilde{A}_{j,1}(x_{1},0)=A_{j,1}^{\ast}(x_{1})
, after the transformation
A_{j,1}(x_{1},t_{1})=:\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(t_{1})}\tilde{A}_{j,1}(x_{1},t_{1})
. As in Step III, we apply the variation of constants formula and split the solution into two parts
\tilde{A}_{j,1}:=\tilde{A}_{j,1,h}+\tilde{A}_{j,1,s}
given by

\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{j,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})&=\mathrm{e}^{t_{1}\Lambda_{1}}A_{j,1}^{\ast}-\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-s_{1})\Lambda_{1}}a_{j,1}^{L}(\tilde{A}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1}),\overline{\tilde{A}_{j,1}}(\cdot,s_{1}))ds_{1},\\
\tilde{A}_{j,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})&=-\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{1}-s_{1})\Lambda_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{1}(s_{1})}\left(a^{N}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1})+4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})+\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\right)ds_{1},\end{split}

(85)

for
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Note that the
\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}
contribution has been included in the source terms, in contrast to Step III. Arguments analogous to those which led to the bound for
\|A_{2,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
in Lemma
5.6
lead to a similar bound for
A_{j,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})=:\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(T_{1})}\tilde{A}_{j,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})
. Specifically, we obtain

\begin{split}\|\tilde{A}_{j,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\|A_{j,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}+Ci\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{-(2\tilde{c}-c)/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\frac{\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}^{2}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2},-(2\tilde{c}-c)\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}}\\
&\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\max_{k=1,\ldots,j}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}},\end{split}

(86)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{j,1}]
, such that

\|A_{j,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|=\mathrm{e}^{I_{1}(T_{1})}\|\tilde{A}_{j,1,h}(\cdot,T_{1})\|\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-c/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}}{{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{3/4}}\max_{k=1,\ldots,j}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}.

We now consider the second term
\tilde{A}_{j,1,s}
. Using the same bound for the semigroup as in Step III we obtain the estimate

\begin{split}\|\tilde{A}_{j,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq\\
C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}&\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{cs_{1}}\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}\left\|a^{N}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1})+4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})+\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\right\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}ds_{1},\end{split}

(87)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{j,1}]
. The
a^{N}_{j,1}
term is controlled by the assumption that
a^{N}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, and the derivative terms
\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})
and
\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})
are easily controlled because we assume sufficient regularity. Specifically,

\begin{split}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\|A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+3}}=C\|A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-1,j}}},\\
\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\|A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+4}}\leq C\|A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-2,j}}},\end{split}

for all
s_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{j,1}]
. Since
A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-1,j}}
and
A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-2,j}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
by the induction hypothesis, it follows that the
H_{ul}^{\theta}
norm in the integrand of (
87
) is bounded above by a constant for all
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. It follows that

\|\tilde{A}_{j,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{cs_{1}}\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}ds_{1}=C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{c/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\Gamma\left(\frac{5}{4},c\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}},

for all
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{j,1}]
, where we used Lemma
A.5
again. After multiplying through by the integrating factor we obtain

\|A_{j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\max_{k=1,\ldots,j}\|A_{k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}+C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-c(t_{1}-1/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\Gamma\left(\frac{5}{4},c\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}-s_{1}\right)\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t_{1}},

(88)

for
t_{1}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{j,1}]
. Since (
88
) is bounded for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
(this follows directly from the asymptotics of the incomplete gamma function and the monotonicity properties of the integral described in Appendix
A
), it follows that
\tilde{T}_{j,1}=T_{1}
, such that
A_{j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
.

We now set
\mu(x)\equiv 0
and assume that the condition (
82
) holds. In order to verify (
83
) we need to improve the bound on the right-most term in (
88
), which is currently
O(1)
at
t_{1}=T_{1}
as
\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}\to 0
. This can be achieved by obtaining better bounds for
\|a^{N}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
,
\|\partial_{x_{3}}^{4}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
and
\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
. It suffices to show that

\|A_{k,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

(89)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
and
k=1,\ldots,j
, where
\tilde{c}=c-C\tilde{K}^{2}\in(0,1)
as before. This is
because (
89
) implies

\|A_{k,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,j}}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}}

for any fixed
\kappa\in(0,\tilde{c})
, for all
k=1,\ldots,j
, assuming that
\zeta>0
is fixed sufficiently small. Thus (
89
) implies (
83
).

For
k=1
, (
89
) follows immediately from Lemma
5.4
. For
k=2
we have that
A_{2,1}=A_{2,1,h}
when
\mu(x)\equiv 0
. Therefore, equation (
78
) in the proof of Lemma
5.6
implies that

\|A_{2,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{2,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

(90)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, as required.

We show (
89
) for
k=3,\ldots,j
by induction. For the base case
k=3
, equation (
82
) and the bounds in (
89
) with
k=1,2
imply that

\|a^{N}_{3,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-3\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{3/4}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}}^{3}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{1/4}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

for
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
(note that
\mathrm{e}^{-3cs_{1}}/(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{3/4}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-cs_{1}}/\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}
on
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
). Since we assume sufficient regularity, bounds for
\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})
and
\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})
are derived directly via the corresponding bounds for
A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})
and
A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})
respectively. Specifically, we have

\begin{split}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}&\leq C\|A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{2,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{2,j}}},\\
\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}&\leq C\|A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}+4}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}(j)}},\end{split}

for
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. We therefore obtain

\big\|a^{N}_{3,1}(\cdot,s_{1})+4i\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{1})+\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\big\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

for
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Combining this with (
87
) leads to

\|\tilde{A}_{3,1,s}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-ct_{1}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{(c-\tilde{c})s_{1}}ds_{1}\\
\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. After multiplying through by the integrating factor we therefore obtain the estimate

\|A_{3,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}\leq C\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\|\tilde{A}_{3,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}+C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}t_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}t_{1}}}\max_{i=1,2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

for
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. The desired bound (
89
) follows after using the bound for
\|\tilde{A}_{3,1,h}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,j}}}
in (
86
), thereby proving the desired result for the base case
k=3
.

It remains to carry out the induction step, for which we assume that (
89
) holds for
k=3,\ldots,j-1\leq N-3
. We present fewer details since the structure of the argument is the same. Using (
82
) it follows that

\|a^{N}_{j,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-3\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{(1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1})^{3/4}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,j-1}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}}^{3}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,j-1}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},

for
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Due to our regularity assumptions and induction hypothesis we have

\begin{split}\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{3}A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\|A_{j-1,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-1,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,j-1}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},\\
\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{4}A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}&\leq C\|A_{j-2,1}(\cdot,s_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{j-2,j}}}\leq C\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{c}s_{1}}}{\sqrt[4]{1-2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}s_{1}}}\max_{i=1,\ldots,j-2}\|A_{i,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{i,j}}},\end{split}

for
s_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. The bound for
\|A_{j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
on
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
then follows analogously to the base case
k=3
. By induction, it follows that (
89
) holds for all
k=3,\ldots,j\leq N-2
. ∎

Remark 5.8
.

In order to verify the assumptions on
a_{j,1}^{N}
in Lemma
5.7
we need to control the norm of non-critical modulation functions
A_{mj,1}
with
|m|\neq 1
. To see that the conditions on
a_{j,1}^{N}
are ‘natural’, recall from Remark
4.6
that
a_{j,1}^{N}
can be written in terms of lower order critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime}}
, where
j^{\prime}<j
. It will follow from Lemma
5.9
and its proof in Section
5.1.1
below that the relevant conditions are always satisfied.

5.1.1
Step V: Estimate
\psi_{1}

Lemmas
5.2
,
5.4
,
5.6
and
5.7
provide sufficient information to characterise the dynamics of the modulation equations (
63
). In order to properly characterise the dynamics of the ‘full approximation’
\psi_{1}
and the map
\pi_{1}
defined in (
64
), however, we must also control the non-critical modulation functions
A_{mj,1}
with
|m|\neq 1
. Moreover, we need to verify the assumptions on
a_{j,1}^{N}
(recall Remark
5.8
). In the following we reinstate the full notation
A_{\pm 1j,1}
instead of
A_{j,1}
in order to distinguish
A_{mj,1}
with
|m|=1
and
|m|\neq 1
.
We also recall that
\textbf{A}_{mj,1}
denotes the set of lower order critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime},1}
with
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,\alpha(m)+j-1\}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\theta_{A}=3(N-3)+\theta
where
\theta>1/2
. We have that
A_{\pm 1j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j,N-2}}
for all
j=1,\ldots,N-2
and
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, and

A_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1})=g_{mj,1}^{gl}\left(\textbf{{A}}_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1}),\varepsilon_{1}(t_{1})\right)\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}},

(91)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
,
m\in I_{N}\setminus\{-1,1\}
and
j=1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)
. If additionally
\mu(x)\equiv 0
then for each
m\in I_{N}\setminus\{-1,1\}
we have

\big\|g_{mj,1}^{gl}\left(\textbf{{A}}_{mj,1}(\cdot,T_{1}),\varepsilon_{1}(T_{1})\right)\big\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\max_{k=1,\ldots,\alpha(m)+j}\|A_{1k,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,N-2}}},

(92)

for all
j=1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)
.

Proof.

Similarly to the established modulation theory for the static SH equation in e.g.
[
82
, Chapter 10]
, this follows from the recursive construction of
\Psi
in Section
4
and the existence of solutions
A_{11,1}\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
due to Lemma
5.4
. The idea is to proceed iteratively with terms of order
O(r_{1}^{\alpha(m)+j})
. For the first three orders, we find the following:

•

\alpha(m)+j=1
: In this case
(m,j)\in\{(\pm 1,1)\}
, and
A_{\pm 11,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
by Lemma
5.4
.

•

\alpha(m)+j=2
: In this case
(m,j)\in\{(\pm 1,2),(0,1),(\pm 2,1)\}
. We have
A_{\pm 21,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{2,N-2}}
by Lemma
5.6
, and
A_{01,1}\equiv A_{\pm 21,1}\equiv 0
after restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{gl}
; recall (
62
).

•

\alpha(m)+j=3
: In this case
(m,j)\in\{(\pm 1,3),(0,2),(\pm 2,2),(\pm 3,1)\}
. Restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{gl}
yields
A_{02,1}=0
,
A_{\pm 22,1}=0
and
A_{\pm 31,1}=-A_{\pm 11,1}^{3}/64
(see again (
62
)). Note that
A_{\pm 31,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
since
A_{\pm 11,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
and
H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
is an algebra. This implies that
a_{3,1}^{N}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. Therefore
A_{\pm 13,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{3,N-2}}
by Lemma
5.7
.

The recursive structure for higher orders is as follows: At the level
\alpha(m)+j=l\leq N
one has a finite set of equations for
A_{mj,1}
with
(m,j)\in\mathcal{I}^{l}:=\{(m,j)\in I_{N}\setminus\{-1,1\}\times\{1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)\}:\alpha(m)+j=l\}
determined via restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{gl}
. The graph equation defining
\mathcal{M}_{1}^{gl}
in (
28
) only depends on
A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime},1}
with
\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}<l
, which in turn depend only on critical modulation functions
A_{\pm 1l^{\prime}}
of lower order with
l^{\prime}<l
; recall Remark
4.6
. In particular,
a_{j,1}^{N}
is a polynomial in modulation functions
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime},1}
with
j^{\prime}<j
. This allows one to show that
a_{j,1}^{N}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j,N-2}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, since Lemmas
5.4
and
5.6
can be used to initiate an inductive proof which relies on the fact that
A_{\pm 1j^{\prime},1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{j^{\prime},N-2}
for all lower orders
j^{\prime}=1,\ldots,j-1
. The details are standard and omitted for brevity. After showing that
a_{j,1}^{N}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j,N-2}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, Lemma
5.7
can be applied to establish that
A_{\pm j,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{j,N-2}}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
.

Assume now that
\mu(x)\equiv 0
, in which case
\nu_{m}=0
for all
m\in I_{N}
and

g_{mj,1}^{gl}\left(\textbf{A}_{mj,1},\varepsilon_{1}\right)=-(\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(0)})^{-1}\bigg(\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(1)}A_{mj-1,1}+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(2)}}A_{mj-2,1}+\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(3)}A_{mj-3,1}+\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(4)}A_{mj-4,1}-a_{mj,1}\bigg).

(93)

In order to prove the bound in (
92
), we note first that the matching condition
\alpha(m_{1})+\alpha(m_{2})+\alpha(m_{3})+j_{1}+j_{2}+j_{3}\leq\alpha(m)+j+2
implies that

\begin{split}\|a_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}\leq C\sup_{\{(m^{\prime},j^{\prime}):\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}\leq\alpha(m)+j-2\}}\|A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime},1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime},N}}},\end{split}

(94)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
and
m\neq 1
. Since assume sufficient regularity to control the derivatives in the linear operators
\mathcal{L}_{m,1}^{(l)}
for
l=1,2,3,4
, we obtain

\begin{split}\|g_{mj,1}^{gl}&\left(\textbf{A}_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1}),\varepsilon_{1}\right)\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}\leq C\bigg(\|A_{mj-1,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j-1,N}+1}}\\
&+\|A_{mj-2,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j-2,N}+2}}+\|A_{mj-3,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j-3,N}+3}}\\
&+\|A_{mj-4,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j-4,N}+4}}+\sup_{\{(m^{\prime},j^{\prime}):\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime}\leq\alpha(m)+j-2\}}\|A_{m^{\prime}j^{\prime},1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m^{\prime})+j^{\prime},N}}}\bigg),\end{split}

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. If we assume as an induction hypothesis that

\|A_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}=\|g_{mj,1}^{gl}\left(\textbf{A}_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1}),\varepsilon_{1}\right)\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}\leq C\sup_{k=1,\ldots,j-2}\|A_{\pm 1k,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,N-2}}}

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
, (the base case follows by the arguments above in dot points), then we obtain

\|g_{mj,1}^{gl}\left(\textbf{A}_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1}),\varepsilon_{1}\right)\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{\alpha(m)+j,N}}}\leq C\sup_{k=1,\ldots,j-2}\|A_{\pm 1k,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k,N-2}}},

(95)

for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. The bound in (
93
) follows after applying the bounds for
A_{\pm 1,j}
when
\mu(x)\equiv 0
from Lemmas
5.4
,
5.6
and (
5.7
) when
\mu(x)\equiv 0
(the condition (
82
) is satisfied due to (
94
)).
∎

Remark 5.10
.

The proof of Lemma
5.9
also shows that the assumptions on
a_{j,1}^{N}
for the applicability of Lemma
5.7
are satisfied.

Combining the results of Lemmas
5.2
,
5.4
,
5.6
,
5.7
and
5.9
we obtain the following result, which summarises the dynamics in chart
\mathcal{K}_{1}
.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
\theta_{A}=1+3(N-3)+\theta
where
\theta>1/2
. Then for
\zeta,K>0
sufficiently small but fixed we have that
\psi_{1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
. In particular, the map
\pi_{1}:\Sigma_{1}^{in}\to\Sigma_{1}^{out}
is well-defined and given by

\pi_{1}:(\psi_{1}^{\ast},\rho_{in}^{1/2},\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})\mapsto\left(\psi_{1}(\cdot,T_{1}),\rho_{in}^{1/2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}{\zeta}\right)^{1/4},\zeta\right),

(96)

where

\psi_{1}(x,T_{1})={\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{1/4}\frac{\rho_{in}^{1/2}}{\zeta^{1/4}}\left(\nu_{1}f_{2,1}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)+{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{1/2}h_{rem,1}(x),

(97)

where
f_{2,1}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})
is the function described in Lemma
5.6
and
h_{rem,1}\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
. If additionally
\mu(x)\equiv 0
then we have

\|\psi_{1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\max_{j=1,\ldots,N-2}\|A_{j,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+1}},

where
\kappa\in(0,\tilde{c})
can be fixed arbitrarily close to
\tilde{c}
if
K,\zeta>0
are sufficiently small.

Proof.

The form of the map in (
96
) follows immediately from Lemma
5.2
. The fact that
\psi_{1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
follows from its definition in (
45
) and the fact that
A_{mj,1}(\cdot,t_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta+1}
for all
m\in I_{N}
with corresponding
j\in\{1,\ldots,\tilde{\alpha}(m)\}
and for all
t_{1}\in[0,T_{1}]
; recall Lemma
5.9
and note that we assume one additional degree of regularity. The extra regularity is needed since we use Lemma
A.1
in order to take the
H_{ul}^{\theta}
with respect to
x
instead of
x_{1}
.

In order to obtain the asymptotic expression in (
97
) we evaluate the series expansion (
45
) at
t_{1}=T_{1}
. This yields

\begin{split}r_{1}(T_{1})\psi_{1}(x,T_{1})&=\sum_{m\in I_{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{\tilde{\alpha}(m)}r_{1}(T_{1})^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,1}(x_{1},T_{1})\mathrm{e}^{imx}\\
&=r_{1}(T_{1})\psi_{GL,1}(x,T_{1})+r_{1}(T_{1})^{2}\left(A_{12,1}(x_{1},T_{1})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)+r_{1}(T_{1})^{3}\tilde{h}_{rem,1}(x,T_{1}),\end{split}

where
\psi_{GL,1}(x,T_{1})=A_{11,1}(x_{1},T_{1})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.
and we used the fact that
A_{01,1}(\cdot,T_{1})=A_{21,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\equiv 0
; recall the proof of Lemma
5.9
. The remainder function satisfies
\tilde{h}_{rem,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
, since it is a linear combination of terms of the form
r_{1}(T_{1})^{\alpha(m)+j}A_{mj,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\mathrm{e}^{imx}
with
r_{1}(T_{1})\leq\rho_{in}^{1/2}
by Lemma
5.2
and
A_{mj,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\in H_{ul}^{\theta+1}
by Lemma
5.9
. The formal leading order approximation
\psi_{GL,1}(x,T_{1})
is in fact higher order, since by Lemmas
5.4
and
A.1
we have

\|\psi_{GL,1}(x,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\|A_{11,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+1}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\|A_{1,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+1}}.

The expression (
97
) follows after substituting the asymptotic expression for
A_{2,1}=A_{12,1}
in Lemma
5.6
, which yields

\begin{split}\psi_{1}(x,T_{1})=r_{1}(T_{1})\left(\nu_{1}f_{2,1}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.\right)+r_{1}(T_{1})^{2}h_{rem,1}(x),\end{split}

(98)

where we defined a new remainder term

h_{rem,1}(x):=r(T_{1})^{-1}(A_{2,1,h}(x_{1},T_{1})\mathrm{e}^{ix}+c.c.)+r_{1}(T_{1})^{-2}\psi_{GL,1}(x,T_{1})+\tilde{h}_{rem,1}(x,T_{1}).

A direct application of the triangle inequality and Lemma
A.1
shows that
h_{rem,1}\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
(note that the norm of
A_{2,1,h}
is exponentially small due to Lemma
5.6
). Substituting the solution
r_{1}(T_{1})=\rho_{in}^{1/2}\sqrt[4]{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}/\zeta}
into (
98
) yields (
97
).

Now assume that
\mu(x)\equiv 0
. It follows from Lemmas
5.4
,
5.6
,
5.7
and
5.9
that for each
m\in I_{N}
and corresponding
j=1,\ldots,N-2
we have

\|A_{mj,1}(\cdot,T_{1})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+1}}\leq C\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa/2\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}\max_{j=1,\ldots,N-2}\|A_{j,1}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta+1}}.

Applying this bound to the series expansion for
\psi_{1}(\cdot,T_{1})
together with a triangle inequality and Lemma
A.1
yields the desired result.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

Recall from Lemma
5.6
that
f_{21}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast})\approx\zeta
for small
\zeta
. Thus a close numerical approximation is
\psi_{1}(x,T_{1})\approx 2\nu_{1}\rho_{in}^{1/2}\zeta^{3/4}{\varepsilon_{1}^{\ast}}^{1/4}\cos x
.

5.2
Chart
\mathcal{K}_{2}

We recall the equations in chart
\mathcal{K}_{2}
:

\begin{split}\partial_{t_{2}}A_{1,2}&=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}A_{1,2}+v_{2}A_{1,2}-3A_{1,2}|A_{1,2}|^{2},\\
\partial_{t_{2}}v_{2}&=1,\\
\partial_{t_{2}}A_{2,2}&=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}A_{2,2}+v_{2}A_{2,2}-a_{2,2}-4i\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}+\nu_{1},\\
\partial_{t_{2}}A_{j,2}&=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}A_{j,2}+v_{2}A_{j,2}-a_{j,2}-4i\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{j-1,2}-\partial_{x_{2}}^{4}A_{j-2,2},\end{split}

(99)

for
j=3,\ldots,N-2
. Our aim in chart
\mathcal{K}_{2}
is to describe the map

\pi_{2}:\Sigma^{in}_{2}\ni\left(\psi_{2}^{\ast},-\zeta^{-1/2},r_{2}\right)\mapsto\left(\psi_{2}(\cdot,T_{2}),\zeta^{-1/2},r_{2}\right)\in\Sigma_{2}^{out},

induced by forward evolution of initial conditions in

\Sigma_{2}^{in}=\left\{(\psi_{2}^{\ast},-\zeta^{-1/2},r_{2}):\|\psi_{2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\zeta^{1/4}K,r_{2}\in[0,\vartheta]\right\},

which corresponds to the exit section
\Sigma_{1}^{out}
in chart
\mathcal{K}_{1}
coordinates (
\vartheta>0
can be chosen such that
\Sigma_{2}^{in}=\kappa_{12}(\Sigma^{out}_{1})
), up to the exit section

\Sigma_{2}^{out}=\left\{(\psi_{2},\rho_{mid},r_{2}):\|\psi_{2}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq\zeta^{1/4}K,r_{2}\in[0,\vartheta]\right\},

which corresponds to the section
\Delta^{mid}
defined in (
36
) after blow-down
after fixing
\varepsilon
and using the natural identification with points in
\Delta^{mid}_{\varepsilon}=\Delta^{mid}\times[0,\varepsilon_{0}]
. The analysis proceeds via steps similar to those of the
\mathcal{K}_{1}
analysis.

Step I: Solve for
v_{2}(t_{2})
and
T_{2}

We have the following result.

Lemma 5.13
.

The transition time is
T_{2}=\zeta^{-1/2}+\rho_{mid}
, and on the interval
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
we have
v_{2}(t_{2})=-\zeta^{-1/2}+t_{2}
.

Proof.

This follows by direct integration and the boundary constraint
v_{2}(T_{2})=\rho_{mid}
.
∎

Step II: Estimate
A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})

We prove the following result.

Lemma 5.14
.

Fix
\theta_{A}>1/2
and assume that
A_{1,2}^{\ast}\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
. Then
A_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
on
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. In particular,

\|A_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}},

(100)

for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
.

Proof.

Substituting the expressions for
v_{2}(t_{2})
into the equation for
A_{1,2}
yields a GL-type equations with time-dependent linear damping:

\partial_{t_{2}}A_{1,2}=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}A_{1,2}+v_{2}(t_{2})A_{1,2}-3A_{1,2}|A_{1,2}|^{2},\qquad A_{1,2}(x_{2},0)=A_{1,2}^{\ast}(x_{2}),

(101)

where
v_{2}(t_{2})=-\zeta^{-1/2}+t_{2}
by Lemma
5.13
. Equation (
101
) decouples from system (
99
), so we may consider it independently. The effect of the non-constant coefficient
v_{2}(t_{2})
can be mitigated via the introduction of an integrating factor
\mathrm{e}^{I_{2}(t_{2})}
, where

I_{2}(t_{2})=\int_{0}^{t_{2}}v_{2}(s_{2})ds_{2}=-\zeta^{-1/2}t_{2}+\frac{t_{2}^{2}}{2}.

(102)

Defining
A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})=:\mathrm{e}^{I_{2}(t_{2})}\tilde{A}_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})
leads to the equation

\partial_{t_{2}}\tilde{A}_{1,2}=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{1,2}-3\mathrm{e}^{2I_{2}(t_{2})}\tilde{A}_{1,2}|A_{1,2}|^{2},\qquad\tilde{A}_{1,2}(x_{2},0)=A_{1,2}^{\ast}(x_{2}),

for which the variation of constants formula yields

\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})=\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}}A_{1,2}^{\ast}-3\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{2}-s_{2})\Lambda_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2I_{2}(s_{2})}\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|^{2}ds_{2},

(103)

where
(\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}})_{t_{2}\geq 0}
is the (heat) semigroup generated by
\Lambda_{2}:=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}
. Since the transition time
T_{2}
is finite, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\mathrm{e}^{2I_{2}(t_{2})}\leq C
for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
A.2
that there is a constant
C>0
such that

\|\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}\to H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C,

for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. Taking the
H^{\theta_{A}}_{ul}
norm in (
103
), we obtain

\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}+3C\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|^{2}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}ds_{2}.

By Lemma
A.3
we have
\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|^{2}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}^{3}\leq\tilde{C}\tilde{K}^{2}\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}
for all
s_{2}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2}]
. Therefore

\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}+\tilde{C}\tilde{K}^{2}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}ds_{2},

for
t_{2}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2}]
. Applying Grönwall’s inequality yields the estimate

\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{C}\tilde{K}^{2}t_{2}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}},

(104)

for
t_{2}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2}]
(
\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{C}\tilde{K}^{2}t_{2}}
is bounded since
\tilde{T}_{2}
is finite). Since (
104
) is finite for all
t_{2}\leq T_{2}
, we have that
A_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\in H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}
for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. The bound in (
100
) follows from (
104
) and the fact that
\|A_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\|\tilde{A}_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}
.
∎

Step III: Estimate
A_{2,2}(x_{2},t_{2})

We prove the following result.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\theta_{A}=3+\theta
where
\theta>1/2
. Then
A_{2,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. Moreover, we have that

A_{2,2}(x_{2},T_{2})=\nu_{1}f_{2,2}+A_{2,2,h}(x_{2},T_{2}),

(105)

where
f_{2,2}
is a constant

f_{2,2}:=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\rho_{mid}^{2}/2}\left(\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{\rho_{mid}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\zeta}}\right)\right)>0,

and

\|A_{2,2,h}(\cdot,T_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}},

(106)

where
\theta_{k}
is given by (
74
).

Proof.

Given solutions for
v_{2}(t_{2})
and
A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})
as described in Lemmas
5.13
and
5.14
respectively, the equation for
A_{2,2}
decouples. We obtain

\partial_{t_{2}}A_{2,2}=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}A_{2,2}+v_{2}(t_{2})A_{2,2}-3A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})^{2}\overline{A_{2,2}}-6|A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})|^{2}A_{2,2}-4i\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})+\nu_{1},

together with
A_{2,2}(x_{2},0)=A_{2,2}^{\ast}(x_{2})
. After applying the transformation
A_{2,2}(x_{2},t_{2})=:\mathrm{e}^{I_{2}(t_{2})}\tilde{A}_{2,2}(x_{2},t_{2})
, where
I_{2}(t_{2})
is the same integrating factor (
102
) as in Step II, we obtain

\partial_{t_{2}}\tilde{A}_{2,2}=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,2}-3A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})^{2}\overline{\tilde{A}_{2,2}}-6|A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})|^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,2}-4i\mathrm{e}^{-I_{2}(t_{2})}\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}(x_{2},t_{2})+\mathrm{e}^{-I_{2}(t_{2})}\nu_{1},

together with
A_{2,2}(x_{2},0)=A_{2,2}^{\ast}(x_{2})
. Again we apply the variation of constants formula and split the solution into two parts via
\tilde{A}_{2,2}:=\tilde{A}_{2,2,h}+\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}
defined by

\begin{split}\tilde{A}_{2,2,h}(\cdot,t_{2})&=\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}}A_{2,2}^{\ast}\ -\\
\int_{0}^{t_{2}}&\mathrm{e}^{(t_{2}-s_{2})\Lambda_{2}}\left(3A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})^{2}\overline{\tilde{A}_{2,2}}(\cdot,s_{2})+6|A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})|^{2}\tilde{A}_{2,2}(\cdot,s_{2})+\mathrm{e}^{-I_{2}(s_{2})}4i\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\right)ds_{2},\\
\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}(\cdot,t_{2})&=\nu_{1}\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{(t_{2}-s_{2})\Lambda_{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-I_{2}(s_{2})}ds_{2},\end{split}

(107)

where as in Step II,
(\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}})_{t_{2}\geq 0}
denotes the heat semigroup generated by
\Lambda_{2}=4\partial_{x_{2}}^{2}
. In order to estimate the norm of
A_{2,2,h}:=\mathrm{e}^{I_{2}}\tilde{A}_{2,2,h}
, first recall from Step II above that
\|\mathrm{e}^{t_{2}\Lambda_{2}}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}\to H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C
for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. Then for all
t_{2}\in[0,\tilde{T}_{2,2}]
, where
\tilde{T}_{2,2}:=\min\{T_{2},\sup\{t_{2}>0:\|\tilde{A}_{2,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{{\theta}}}\leq\tilde{M}\}\}
for an arbitrarily large but fixed constant
\tilde{M}>0
(c.f. Remark
5.5
), we obtain

\|\tilde{A}_{2,2,h}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\|A_{2,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}+C\tilde{M}^{2}T_{2}\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}\leq C\max_{k=1,2}\|A_{k,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{k}}},

(108)

where we used Lemma
A.3
, the fact that
\|A_{1,2}(\cdot,t_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\tilde{K}\|A_{1,2}^{\ast}\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}
for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
due to Lemma
5.14
, and
\mathrm{e}^{-I_{2}(s_{2})}\|\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\|\partial_{x_{2}}^{3}A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta}}\leq C\|A_{1,2}(\cdot,s_{2})\|_{H_{ul}^{\theta_{A}}}
in order to derive the the right-hand side. Since (
108
) is bounded for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
and the integrating factor is bounded,
A_{2,2,h}(\cdot,t_{2})\in H_{ul}^{\theta}
and satisfies (
106
) for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
.

We can calculate
\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}
explicitly by viewing it as the solution to a heat equation with a constant source term
\nu_{1}
and initial data
\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}(x_{2},0)\equiv 0
. A direct calculation using the explicit solution formula gives the spatially homogeneous expression

\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}(x_{2},t_{2})=\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}(t_{2})=\nu_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{1/2\zeta}\left(\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\zeta}t_{2}-1}{\sqrt{2\zeta}}\right)+\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\zeta}}\right)\right),

for all
t_{2}\in[0,T_{2}]
. In particular,

\tilde{A}_{2,2,s}(T_{2})=\nu_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathrm{e}^{1/2\zeta}\left(\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{\rho_{mid}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\textnormal{erf}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\zeta}}\right)\right).

The expression in (
105
) follows after multiplying through by the integrating factor.
∎

Step IV: Estimate
A_{j,2}(x_{2},t_{2})
,
j=3,\ldots,N-2

In order to consider modulation functions
A_{j,2}
with
j\geq 3
we use Lemma
4.3
again in order to separate
a_{j,2}=:a_{j,2}^{L}+a_{j,2}^{N}
into two parts. Similarly to the analysis in chart
\mathcal{K}_{1}
Step IV, we write
